coordinator · Research baby-science facts for lyrics11:16 PM

You are researching primary scientific literature for a fact sheet that will feed a dense, fact-packed song about infant science (think Hamilton-style lyrical density). Deliverable: return your findings as your final message in Markdown (do NOT write any files). Use WebSearch and WebFetch (load them via ToolSearch "select:WebSearch,WebFetch" first).

Themes you own: SLEEP, CRYING, FEEDING, GROWTH, SENSES. For each fact, give: one or two lines, the specific number(s), the primary paper (first author, year, journal) or best authoritative source, a URL, and a reliability flag: [solid] / [contested] / [pop-science-only]. Prefer primary papers and university press releases over blogs. Verify numbers; do not invent them. If you cannot verify a number, say so explicitly.

Items to verify and quantify (add others you find striking):

SLEEP

  • Newborn sleep: hours per day (16-18?), percent REM/active sleep in newborns (~50%) vs adults (~20-25%), when REM proportion drops; Roffwarg, Muzio & Dement 1966 Science ontogenetic hypothesis.
  • Rebecca Spencer (UMass Amherst): naps consolidate memory in preschoolers (Kurdziel, Duclos & Spencer 2013 PNAS) - effect sizes, % forgetting without nap; her later infant work; she appeared in Netflix "Babies" Sleep episode - what did she say / what study was filmed (press release UMass Feb 2020).
  • Mark Blumberg (Iowa): sleep twitches in infants (REM twitches as sensorimotor training) - key paper and finding; was he in the Netflix series?
  • 4-month sleep architecture change (emergence of adult-like sleep stages, circadian consolidation); "sleep regressions" at 4/8/12 months - what does the evidence actually say? (Is there peer-reviewed evidence for discrete regressions? Hysing, Mindell, Galland reference ranges.)
  • Infant sleep variability: Galland et al. 2012 Sleep Medicine Reviews normative values (e.g., 0-2 months total sleep ~14.6 h, range); Paavonen/ Hysing infant night wakings % at 6/12 months; Pennestri 2018 Pediatrics "sleeping through the night" (27-57% of 6-12-month-olds don't).
  • Sleep and synaptic pruning/ brain development claims.

CRYING

  • Normal crying curve: peaks ~6 weeks at ~2-3 h/day, declines by 3-4 months (Brazelton 1962 Pediatrics; Barr's "Period of PURPLE Crying"); St James-Roberts; Wolke et al. 2017 J Pediatrics meta-analysis of fuss/cry durations by country (e.g., Denmark/Germany/Japan lowest, UK/Canada/Italy highest; numbers).
  • Colic definition (Wessel rule of 3s: 3 h/day, 3 days/week, 3 weeks) and prevalence.
  • Cry acoustics: newborn cry melody shaped by native language prosody (Mampe, Friederici, Christophe & Wermke 2009 Current Biology: French vs German newborns), 30-60 cries.
  • Shaken baby / crying as trigger - a sentence only.
  • Do babies cry with tears? (tear production onset ~1-3 months.)

FEEDING

  • Colostrum composition/amount (teaspoons in first days), stomach size of newborn (the "cherry/walnut/egg" claim - how solid is it? check Bergman 2013 and criticism).
  • Katie Hinde (Arizona State): rhesus macaque milk differs by infant sex (Hinde 2009 Am J Hum Biol / Hinde 2013-14 cortisol in milk; sons get richer milk, daughters more volume/calcium) - numbers; was she in Netflix "Babies"?
  • Human milk composition changes across the day (melatonin/cortisol), during a feed (foremilk/hindmilk), and over lactation; immune cells spike during infant illness (Hassiotou 2013; Riskin 2012).
  • Mennella: amniotic fluid and breast milk carry flavors (Mennella, Jagnow & Beauchamp 2001 Pediatrics: carrot juice study, mothers drank 300 ml carrot juice; babies showed fewer negative faces); garlic 1991/1993.
  • Newborn taste: sweet preference at birth, salt taste emerges ~4 months, bitter rejection; Steiner facial expression studies.
  • Microbiome seeding by delivery mode: Dominguez-Bello 2010 PNAS (vaginal vs C-section babies' microbiota resemble mother's vaginal vs skin), 2016 Nature Medicine vaginal seeding; 2019 Nature Shao et al. (Baby Biome Study, 596 babies, C-section lacked Bacteroides, more hospital pathogens); was Dominguez-Bello in Netflix Babies "First Food"?
  • Michael Georgieff (Minnesota): iron and brain development - was he in Netflix Babies? Key finding.
  • Introduction of solids / allergen timing (LEAP study 2015 NEJM: early peanut introduction reduced allergy ~80% relative risk reduction).
  • Breastfeeding and IQ evidence (PROBIT, Victora 2015 Lancet Global Health Brazil cohort +3.76 IQ points; sibling studies) - flag as contested.

GROWTH

  • Michelle Lampl (Emory): saltatory growth - Lampl, Veldhuis & Johnson 1992 Science: infants grew 0.5-2.5 cm in bursts separated by 2-63 days of no growth; daily measurement of 31 infants. Netflix Babies "Crawling" episode featured Lampl - what was shown (Emory press release Feb 2020)? Also Lampl's sleep-growth link (Lampl & Johnson 2011 Sleep: growth spurts follow increased sleep).
  • Weight doubling by ~5 months, tripling by 1 year; length +50% by 1 year; head circumference growth; brain reaches ~80% of adult volume by age 2 (Knickmeyer 2008 J Neurosci: brain volume +101% in year 1, +15% in year 2).
  • 270-300 bones at birth fusing to 206 - how solid? Fontanelle closure timing (anterior ~18 months, posterior ~2 months).
  • Synapse formation: "up to 1 million new neural connections per second" (Harvard Center on the Developing Child claim - trace the source and how solid it is; Huttenlocher synaptic density peaks).
  • Birth weight; proportion of body that is head at birth (~1/4 of body length).
  • Teething: first tooth ~6 months; no, teething does not cause fever (Massignan 2016 Pediatrics meta-analysis).

SENSES

  • Newborn visual acuity (~20/400 or 20/600; Snellen equivalents; reaches 20/20 by ~3-5 years), focal distance 8-12 inches, color vision emergence (~2-3 months; red first), newborn preference for faces (Johnson & Morton 1991; Goren 1975), preference for mother's face within days (Bushnell 1989), newborns prefer high-contrast.
  • Hearing: fetus hears from ~24-27 weeks; DeCasper & Fifer 1980 Science (newborns suck to hear mother's voice; "Cat in the Hat" DeCasper & Spence 1986); newborns prefer native language (Moon, Cooper & Fifer 1993); Moon 2013 Acta Paediatrica vowel learning in utero.
  • Smell: newborns prefer mother's amniotic fluid/breast odor (Varendi, Porter & Winberg 1994 Lancet; Marlier); newborns crawl toward unwashed breast.
  • Touch/pain: newborns feel pain (Goksan 2015 eLife fMRI 18 of 20 adult pain regions active); skin-to-skin effects.
  • Synesthesia-like cross-modal perception in infants; infants' pupil / eye-contact findings.
  • Infant visual system: newborns see mostly in shades of gray? (Verify.)
  • Babies' preference for sweet; taste buds count claim (10,000?).

Return a dense, well-organized Markdown list grouped under SLEEP / CRYING / FEEDING / GROWTH / SENSES, ~120-180 lines, each fact with number + citation + URL + reliability flag. Note explicitly which Netflix "Babies" scientists you confirmed via press releases (quote the episode name if found).

The search confirmed the paper's citation and its general significance, but it did not return the specific "50 percent" newborn REM figure, so that detail remains unverified here.

- **Citation:** Roffwarg, Muzio, and Dement published "Ontogenetic development of the human sleep-dream cycle" in *Science* in 1966, vol. 152, no. 3722, pp. 604–619 (Roffwarg, HP; Muzio, JN; Dement, WC (1966). "Ontogenetic development of the human sleep-dream cycle". Science. 152 (3722): 604–619.). DOI: 10.1126/science.152.3722.604.
- **Core idea:** The paper's abstract argues that dreaming sleep early in life may mainly serve the development of the central nervous system (The prime role of "dreaming sleep" in early life may be in the development of the central nervous system.).
- **Legacy:** The paper launched the "ontogenetic hypothesis" linking REM sleep to neural plasticity and development. A 2024 review says the evidence to date suggests that Roffwarg's ontogenesis hypothesis of sleep and brain development is correct, though a 2023 tribute notes much is still unknown.
- **Gap:** None of the results give the newborn REM proportion (about 50 percent of sleep). To verify it, check the full text of the 1966 paper or a review that quotes it.

The search confirmed the study's basic findings but did not return a specific percentage for the memory results.

- **Study:** Kurdziel, Duclos, and Spencer's paper appeared in PNAS in 2013 and is titled "Sleep spindles in midday naps enhance learning in preschool children." The PubMed abstract reports that classroom naps support learning in preschool children by enhancing memories acquired earlier in the day compared with equivalent intervals spent awake.
- **Sample:** One summary says the researchers recruited 40 children from six preschools across western Massachusetts.
- **Spindles:** The PubMed abstract says physiological recordings of naps support a role of sleep spindles in memory performance. A Cognitive Neuroscience Society summary adds that spindle density increased during naps, and seemed responsible for the enhanced learning in a visuo-spatial task.
- **Habitual nappers:** The abstract also reports that this nap benefit is greatest for children who nap habitually, regardless of age.

For the exact percentages, the full text on PubMed Central (PMC3808582) is the place to look. The results section should give the group comparison figures.

Rebecca Spencer, a professor in psychological and brain sciences, is featured in a new Netflix series called "Babies," which debuts on Friday, Feb. 21 on the streaming service. The release dates the Netflix show's filming to a UMass visit: A film crew from the London-based producer Nutopia visited the UMass campus in March 2019 to film Spencer working with infants and their parents in her sleep lab.

Links: [{"title":"exploring origins and functions sleep premature infants","url":"https://stories.uiowa.edu/exploring-origins-and-functions-sleep-premature-infants"},{"title":"sleepreviewmag.com","url":"https://sleepreviewmag.com/?p=392032"},{"title":"human infant brains sleep stage","url":"https://sleepreviewmag.com/sleep-health/demographics/human-infant-brains-sleep-stage/"},{"title":"Study of babies' twitches may provide insights into neurodevelopmental disorders like autism and schizophrenia","url":"https://www.mdlinx.com/article/study-of-babies-twitches-may-provide-insights-into-neurodevelopmental-disorders-like-autism-and/lfc-524"},{"title":"www.sciencedaily.com","url":"https://www.sciencedaily.com/releases/2021/06/210616113827.htm"},{"title":"what s going on when babies twitch in their sleep2","url":"https://www.newswise.com/articles/what-s-going-on-when-babies-twitch-in-their-sleep2"},{"title":"University of Iowa team studies sleep in premature infants","url":"https://iowacapitaldispatch.com/?p=47287"},{"title":"blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu","url":"https://blumberg.lab.uiowa.edu/node/41"},{"title":"sleep twitches may linked sensorimotor development","url":"https://sleepreviewmag.com/sleep-health/demographics/age/sleep-twitches-may-linked-sensorimotor-development/"},{"title":"Baby Mammals Twitch in Their Sleep Because They’re Learning Cute Moves","url":"https://jezebel.com/baby-mammals-twitch-in-their-sleep-because-they-re-lear-540889903"}]

Mark Blumberg, a University of Iowa psychology professor, has studied infant sleep twitches for about two decades. The results support these points:

- **Netflix:** The Blumberg lab's site lists its research on infant sleep as highlighted in Episode 5 of the Netflix series *Babies*. The results don't give further detail on the episode.
- **Premature infant study:** A 2025 University of Iowa story describes a five-year, $3.1 million study funded by the National Institute of Child Health and Human Development and led by Blumberg. It recruits hundreds of preterm infants in the NICU and compares their sleep, twitches, and brain activity with those of full-term infants.
- **Twitches and brain development:** Blumberg's team argues that twitches during REM sleep help shape sensorimotor development. A 2016 MDLinx article reports they see these twitches as the body activating brain circuits and teaching infants about their limbs.
- **Twitching outside REM sleep:** A 2021 ScienceDaily report says that beginning around three months of age, infants show a pronounced increase in twitching during quiet sleep. Blumberg called this surprising and possibly unique to humans.

Links: [{"title":"Barbara Galland","url":"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbara_Galland"},{"title":"Galland (2012) Normal sleep patterns in infants and children","url":"https://www.nationaljewish.org/NJH/media/pdf/Meltzer%20References/Galland-(2012)-Normal-sleep-patterns-in-infants-and-children.pdf"},{"title":"Normal sleep patterns in infants and children: a systematic review of observational studies","url":"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21784676/"},{"title":"normal sleep patterns in infants and children a sy","url":"https://www.em-consulte.com/article/706135/normal-sleep-patterns-in-infants-and-children-a-sy"},{"title":"normal sleep patterns in infants and children a sy","url":"https://em-consulte.com/article/706135/figures/normal-sleep-patterns-in-infants-and-children-a-sy"},{"title":"Normal Sleep Patterns in Infants and Children: A Systematic Review of Observational Studies","url":"https://apedia.attachmentparenting.org/book/export/html/1742"},{"title":"Sleep disorders","url":"https://ijpp.in/Files/2024/ver2/Sleep-disorders.pdf"},{"title":"Normal sleep development 2020","url":"https://trepo.tuni.fi/bitstream/handle/10024/121030/Normal_sleep_development_2020.pdf?sequence=2"},{"title":"Çocuklarda Normal Uyku","url":"https://mail.books.akademisyen.net/index.php/akya/catalog/book/1351/chapter/19102"}]

The 2012 paper is Galland, Taylor, Elder and Herbison, "Normal sleep patterns in infants and children: a systematic review of observational studies," in *Sleep Medicine Reviews* 16(3), 213–222. I could not find the "0–2 months, 14.6 hours" figure in the results.

- **Scope and method:** The review covered ages 0–12 years and followed PRISMA guidelines. It extracted mean and variability data for sleep duration, night wakings, sleep latency, longest overnight sleep period, and daytime naps from 34 eligible studies.
- **Sleep duration reference values:** The abstract reports means with ±1.96 SD ranges for infants (12.8 hours, range 9.7–15.9), toddlers/preschoolers (11.9 hours), and children (9.2 hours) (infant, 12.8 (9.7-15.9); toddler/preschool, 11.9 (9.9-13.8); and child, 9.2 (7.6-10.8)). The 12.8-hour infant mean is the closest match to your number, but it is a broad infant band, not a 0–2 month figure.
- **Night wakings:** The data were split into age bands up to 2 years, with 0 to 3.4 wakes per night for infants (0-2 months).
- **Caveats:** The authors describe the reference values as global norms because they pooled data across countries and cultures. A meta-regression also found that predominantly Asian countries reported about one hour less sleep across the 0–12 year range.

If you need the exact 0–2 month duration, check the full text or the paper's tables, since the abstract does not give it.

Links: [{"title":"experts.mcmaster.ca","url":"https://experts.mcmaster.ca/scholarly-works/1607157"},{"title":"Study of close to 400 infants found no association between interrupted sleep and later developmental problems","url":"https://healthenews.mcgill.ca/parents-shouldnt-worry-if-their-infant-doesnt-sleep-through-the-night-by-6-12-months-of-age/"},{"title":"www.mcgill.ca","url":"https://www.mcgill.ca/edu-ecp/node/2673"},{"title":"See What HealthDay Can Do For You","url":"https://consumer.healthday.com/pediatrics-15/infant-and-child-care-health-news-410/many-6-month-12-month-olds-do-not-sleep-through-the-night-739481.html"},{"title":"Jury still out on infant sleep importance","url":"https://community.the-hospitalist.org/index.php/content/nearly-half-infants-dont-sleep-through-night-1-year"},{"title":"Jury still out on infant sleep importance","url":"https://blogs.the-hospitalist.org/index.php/content/nearly-half-infants-dont-sleep-through-night-1-year"},{"title":"Jury still out on infant sleep importance","url":"https://blogs.the-hospitalist.org/content/nearly-half-infants-dont-sleep-through-night-1-year"},{"title":"nearly half infants dont sleep through night 1 year","url":"https://mdedge.com/content/nearly-half-infants-dont-sleep-through-night-1-year"},{"title":"User login","url":"https://community.the-hospitalist.org/content/nearly-half-infants-dont-sleep-through-night-1-year"},{"title":"mdedge.com","url":"https://mdedge9-ma1.mdedge.com/node/184226/path_term/50252"}]

The Pennestri et al. study (Pediatrics, 2018) found that a large share of healthy infants had not reached uninterrupted night sleep by 6 or 12 months, and that this was not linked to later development.

- **Sample and design:** The study used 388 mother-infant pairs from the MAVAN birth cohort in Canada. Dr. Pennestri and colleagues reported on 388 mother-infant dyads in a longitudinal birth cohort study called Maternal Adversity, Vulnerability, and Neurodevelopment (MAVAN).
- **Prevalence:** Mothers reported sleep at 6 and 12 months. Using a definition of either 6 or 8 hours of uninterrupted sleep, the authors found that 27.9% to 57.0% of 6- and 12-month-old infants did not sleep through the night. Specific figures: at 6 months, 62.4% of infants attained at least 6 hours of uninterrupted sleep, while 43.0% had reached 8 hours. At 12 months, 72.1% of the infants attained 6 hours, and 56.6% attained 8 hours.
- **Development:** Bayley-II scores were measured at 6, 12, and 36 months. Linear regressions revealed no significant associations between sleeping through the night and concurrent or later mental development, psychomotor development, or maternal mood (P > .05).
- **Breastfeeding:** The rate of breastfeeding was significantly higher among infants who did not sleep through the night.
- **Conclusion:** The authors suggested that expectations for early sleep consolidation could be moderated.

Caveat: the study relies on maternal report, and I did not confirm the exact percentages in the full article text. The 27.9% and 57.0% range comes from the abstract, and the 6- and 12-month figures come from a secondary news summary. Check the paper (doi 10.1542/peds.2017-4330) before citing exact numbers.

The Wolke et al. (2017) meta-analysis in the Journal of Pediatrics pooled 28 diary studies covering 8,690 infants. It found that fussing and crying averaged about 117–133 minutes a day in the first six weeks and fell to about 68 minutes by 10–12 weeks. Of 5687 articles reviewed, 28 diary studies (33 samples) were suitable for inclusion in meta-analysis, and the authors searched Medline, PsycINFO, and Embase.

On the countries you asked about:
- **Denmark and Japan** had the lowest colic rates. Colic was much more frequent in the first 6 weeks (17%-25%) compared with 11% by 8-9 weeks of age and 0.6% by 10-12 weeks of age, according to modified Wessel criteria and lowest in Denmark and Japan.
- **Denmark** had the lowest fuss/cry durations at nearly all age points. One source says Danish babies are the mellowest, with the lowest "fuss/cry durations" at every age except 8–9 weeks, where data were insufficient.
- **Germany** had the shortest crying at 1–2 weeks, at 69 minutes versus an average of 117.3 minutes, per the same source.
- **Canada, the UK, and Italy** were among the higher-crying countries. Canadian infants at 3–4 weeks showed longer crying and a higher share of colic, and other "high crying" countries included the Netherlands and the United Kingdom.
- **Denmark** and **Germany** were the quietest at the early ages in one report.

Caveats the authors and reporters noted: the studies were unevenly distributed across countries, some age groups had sparse data, and many studies used different statistical methods, which makes cross-country comparisons harder. The analysis also found no statistical evidence for a universal crying peak at 6 weeks of age across studies, though a later meta-analysis (PMC9541248) covering infancy to 12 months reported a pooled peak of about 126 minutes at 5–6 weeks.

The sample matches your description. The team recorded and analyzed the cries of 60 healthy newborns, 30 born into French-speaking families and 30 born into German-speaking families. The reported age differs by source: ScienceDaily gives three to five days old, while Max Planck's summary says two to five days. The core finding is that French newborns tend to cry with a rising melody contour, whereas German newborns seem to prefer a falling melody contour. The researchers tied this to characteristic differences between the two languages.

A blog analysis reports the underlying data as 1254 cries (selected from 2500 recordings) from 30 French and 30 German monolingual families. It also notes that in mean there was a significant difference between the melody of German baby cries and of French baby cries, even though individual babies in both groups varied widely. Treat that blog as secondary; I did not verify these figures against the paper itself.

The 1992 paper is confirmed: Lampl, Veldhuis and Johnson, "Saltation and stasis: a model of human growth," *Science* 258(5083): 801-803. The abstract reports that infant length grew in discrete spurts rather than continuously. The spurts measured 0.5 to 2.5 cm, separated by intervals of no measurable growth lasting 2 to 63 days. **Sample:** 40 children (31 female; mean age 49.76 months) completed both conditions. Average classroom nap length was 77.71 minutes (SD = 18.75).

- Baseline (immediate recall) did not differ between conditions, t(39) = 0.351, P = 0.728.

**Next-day (24-hour) recall:**
- The nap advantage persisted: "performance in the nap condition remained superior when recall was again probed the following morning," t(22) = 2.824, P = 0.01.

- Negative correlation with immediate recall: r = −0.665, P = 0.010.
- Positive correlation with change in memory over the nap: r = 0.647, P = 0.012.
- Adjusted change in recall: r = 0.693, P = 0.006.

**Habitual nappers:**
- Habitual nappers (n = 17, five or more naps per week) showed a significant nap benefit: t(16) = 2.561, P = 0.021.
- Nonhabitual nappers (n = 10, fewer than two naps per week) showed no benefit: t(9) = 0.347, P = 0.736.
- Immediate recall was similar between groups (M = 73.5 vs. 72.9).

Links: [{"title":"Sleep as a driver of pre- and postnatal brain development","url":"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC11624135/"},{"title":"Rapid Eye Movement Sleep during Early Life: A Comprehensive Narrative Review","url":"https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9602694/"},{"title":"s41390 024 03371 5","url":"https://link.springer.com/10.1038/s41390-024-03371-5"},{"title":"Journal article","url":"https://rex.libraries.wsu.edu/esploro/outputs/journalArticle/The-ontogeny-of-mammalian-sleep-a/99900547149801842"},{"title":"pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov","url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9602694"},{"title":"pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov","url":"https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6220670/figure/F1"},{"title":"Next Article in Journal","url":"https://www.mdpi.com/1660-4601/19/20/13101/review_report"},{"title":"dspace.library.uu.nl","url":"https://dspace.library.uu.nl/handle/1874/458139"},{"title":"Rapid Eye Movement Sleep during Early Life: A Comprehensive Narrative Review","url":"https://integ04-cnspfv.archimed.fr/doc/OAI_1/oai-HAL-hal-04599577v1"}]

The search turned up a review that matches your figures closely, plus a critical reappraisal of the hypothesis.

**Proportions:** A 2022 narrative review reports that full-term newborns spend about half their sleep time in REM, and that the REM share falls with age until it levels off near 20% of total sleep by around age three. That plateau holds through adulthood, with a slight decline in late life (Full-term newborns spend one-third of their day and one-half of their sleep in REM sleep; the percentage in REM sleep over total sleep time progressively declines with age and reaches a roughly stable proportion of 20% in REM sleep). The same review gives higher prenatal figures, with REM at roughly 80% at 30 weeks and 58% at 36 to 38 weeks (REM sleep is progressively reduced from 80% at 30 weeks to 67% between 33 and 35 weeks, and further to 58% between 36 and 38 weeks).

**Roffwarg's hypothesis:** Roffwarg proposed in 1966 that REM sleep supports early neural development. A 2024 review in *Pediatric Research* says the evidence to date supports the ontogenic hypothesis, though the link to functional connectivity still needs work (The evidence to date suggests that Roffwarg's ontogenesis hypothesis of sleep and brain development is correct.). A separate 2022 review describes the abundant early REM as likely optimizing conditions for neuronal differentiation, migration, myelination and synapse formation (The abundant REM sleep appears to serve to optimize a cerebral state suitable for homeostasis and inherent neuronal activities favorable to brain maturation).

**Caveat:** Frank and Heller (2003) challenge the premise that newborn sleep is truly REM. Their review argues that REM sleep may not be present at birth in these species; rather, it appears that all mammals early in development exhibit spontaneous, dissociated activity. This is a critique of the animal data, mainly altricial mammals, not of the human proportions above.

Note that the 50% figure comes from a single review's summary of older polysomnographic work, so check the primary Roffwarg et al. (1966) data if you need exact values.

I ran one search, and it did not turn up a peer-reviewed study on the 4-month sleep regression. Most results were parent-facing health blogs and clinic pages, so they don't settle the question.

- **The only data-based source is secondary.** Emily Oster's ParentData piece reports that, in infant-tracking data, the pattern expected from a 4-month regression does not appear sharply. She notes that if the regression were real, shorter sleep periods and more night wakings would show up at that age, and that pattern doesn't appear starkly in the data. That is a commentary on data, not a peer-reviewed paper, so it's worth checking the underlying studies.
- **Night-to-night variability is large.** Oster also cites a study finding that nighttime sleep can easily vary by two hours on a day-to-day basis, which could make a single bad week look like a regression.
- **Clinical and parenting sites mostly treat it as real.** One site says the 4 month sleep regression is real, though it is better described as a permanent shift in how babies sleep rather than a temporary setback. Another notes that "sleep regression" isn't a term used in medical care.
- **Alternative explanations exist.** Sites list illness, reflux, teething, growth spurts, and schedule problems as causes of similar symptoms, so a change in sleep isn't automatically a regression.
- **Some results are low quality.** Several near-identical pages (Flashtelecom, Blask, Dachbleche24, and others) appear to be reposted content, and their claims about "well-documented" periods aren't sourced. I wouldn't rely on them.

For a proper evidence base, the next step would be searching PubMed or Google Scholar directly for longitudinal infant sleep studies that examine age-related changes in night waking or sleep consolidation around 3 to 5 months.

Berry Brazelton","url":"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T._Berry_Brazelton"},{"title":"ictes.chmnu.edu.ua","url":"https://ictes.chmnu.edu.ua/?p=22781"},{"title":"the crying infant","url":"https://www.em-consulte.com/es/article/611904/the-crying-infant"},{"title":"Un problema frecuente en el consultorio | 10 AGO 18","url":"https://www.intramed.net/92881?pagina=3"},{"title":"crying baby emergency just normal infant behavior","url":"https://acepnow.com/article/crying-baby-emergency-just-normal-infant-behavior"},{"title":"fa.hms.harvard.edu","url":"https://fa.hms.harvard.edu/resource/memorialminutebrazeltontberrypdf"},{"title":"www.wral.com","url":"https://www.wral.com/archive/17417577/"},{"title":"12 P49 57","url":"https://eprints.lib.hokudai.ac.jp/repo/huscap/all/25256/12_P49-57.pdf"}]

The search confirms the study and most of your details, with one discrepancy in the hours figure.

- **Sample and design:** The abstract describes eighty mothers of normal newborn infants kept daily records of their infants' fussing for the first 12 weeks. Brazelton's own memorial sketch says the sample was 80 mothers of healthy full-term newborn infants.
- **Hours per day:** The abstract reports an average of 2¼ hours' crying in the first 7 weeks, with less each week thereafter. The Harvard memorial also gives 2½ hours daily crying in the first 7 weeks. Your figure of 2¾ hours at six weeks comes from a secondary source, ACEP Now, which says the median peaks at 2.75 hours a day at six weeks of age. I could not find the 2¾ figure in the primary abstract, so check the full paper before citing it.
- **Other details:** The ACEP article also reports a median of 1.75 hours a day at two weeks of age and less than one hour of crying a day by 12 weeks.
- **Unreliable source:** One result (ictes.chmnu.edu.ua) says crying peaked around six months of age. That conflicts with the other sources and appears to be a low-quality site, so I would not rely on it.

The full article is at doi 10.1542/peds.29.4.579 (Pediatrics 29:579–588, 1962). The Lancashire repository copy is restricted to registered users.

The search didn't return the 1954 Wessel paper itself, but several sources describe its criteria and the prevalence figures reported under them.

**Criteria:** Wessel's "rule of threes" defines colic as crying more than 3 hours a day, on more than 3 days a week, for at least 3 weeks (Dr. Wessel defined colic by the amount of crying: >3 hours a day, >3 days a week, and lasting at least 3 weeks.). One source notes that many clinicians do not strictly adhere to the criterion of persistence for >3 weeks.

**Prevalence, using Wessel criteria in primary studies:**
- A Pakistani cohort found 90 infants (21.77%) satisfied the Wessel criteria for infantile colic.
- An Iranian cohort found 65 infants (20.24%) satisfied the Wessel criteria for infantile colic.

**Prevalence across reviews and estimates varies widely:**
- A review by Samara and colleagues notes that definitions for colic vary widely ranging from gastrointestinal symptoms to inconsolable crying, which has resulted in variations of the prevalence rate from 1.5% to 11.9%.
- Another source gives estimates of cumulative incidence have varied from 10% to 40%.
- A Ukrainian article reports ranges of 5-19% of children of the first months of life in some sources, 10-20% in others, and some claims as high as 70–90%.
- A popular article cites approximate 20 to 25 percent of babies, but this is not a peer-reviewed estimate.

**Bottom line:** There's no single agreed percentage. Figures under Wessel criteria in individual cohorts cluster around 20%, and reported prevalence across the literature ranges from roughly 1.5% to 40%, depending on the definition used. The Samara review's figure is the most relevant systematic-style estimate in these results, but I didn't find a pooled rate for Wessel criteria specifically. I'd recommend checking the original 1954 Pediatrics paper and the Samara review directly for exact figures.

The search didn't turn up a peer-reviewed study. The most reliable result is a Healthline article, and it points to roughly 1 to 3 months for visible crying tears.

- **Healthline (most reliable here):** Newborns cry dry-eyed for the first couple of weeks. Their lacrimal glands start ramping up production around 2 weeks, but parents may not notice much change yet. Visible tears typically appear sometime between 1 and 3 months. It recommends a pediatrician visit if a baby still isn't producing tears after 3 months.

Overall, the consensus is that visible crying tears usually start within the first 2 to 3 months, with individual variation. Note that most sources blur reflex tears (from irritation) and emotional tears, and I found no direct study of the lacrimal reflex. If you need citable research, a search on pediatric ophthalmology journals or the AAP would be the next step.

I couldn't find the 2009 *American Journal of Human Biology* paper itself in these results, so the details below come from later papers and press coverage, not the 2009 article.

- **Milk volume and fat/protein (2009 finding, via press):** Coverage of Hinde's work reports that mothers of daughters produced more milk, a finding subsequently replicated in cows. The same coverage says the milk produced for sons is richer in fat and protein. One report adds that the fat richness was especially when it's mom's first birth.
- **Calcium (2013 paper):** A 2013 *American Journal of Physical Anthropology* study, "Daughter dearest," found that mothers rearing daughters tended to produce higher mean Ca concentration in milk, and consequently a higher Ca/P ratio, than did mothers rearing sons, even though protein concentration was not elevated. Press coverage links this to faster female skeletal development, with daughters need[ing] more minerals, sooner, because they grow faster.
- **Cortisol (later studies):** A larger study of 108 mother–infant pairs found that glucocorticoids in mother's milk, independent of available milk energy, predicted a more Nervous, less Confident temperament in both sons and daughters. Another earlier study reported that a mother rhesus monkey had higher cortisol levels in her breast milk if she had a son instead of a daughter. Also, higher cortisol concentrations in milk were associated with greater infant weight gain across time.

To verify the 2009 paper's specific claims, you'd need to check the article directly in *American Journal of Human Biology*; the search didn't return it.

The Massignan et al. meta-analysis (Pediatrics, 2016) found that signs and symptoms are common during primary tooth eruption, but it did not classify the temperature rises as fever.

- **Main findings:** The pooled prevalence of any sign or symptom was 70.5% across 16 studies of children aged 0 to 36 months (3,506 children). The most common were gingival irritation (86.81%), irritability (68.19%), and drooling (55.72%). (Based upon 16 included studies, the researchers found that the overall prevalence of signs and symptoms occurring during primary tooth eruption in children between 0 and 36 months was 70.5 percent (total sample, 3,506); gingival irritation (86.81 percent), irritability (68.19 percent), and drooling (55.72 percent).)
- **Temperature:** The authors reported that eruption could raise body temperature, but that the rise did not meet the definition of fever. (For body temperature analyses, eruption could lead to a rise in temperature, but it was not characterized as fever.)
- **Limitations:** A later review (Matos and Silva, 2018) cited Massignan et al. as finding a slight temperature increase without fever. It also noted that the included studies varied widely in how they defined fever and measured temperature, so the evidence is not conclusive. (The present analysis had several limitations, including a significant heterogeneity amongst studies in the definition of fever, interpersonal variability during fever measurements, different fever assessment methods or absence of that information.)
- **Quality concerns:** Of the 16 studies, 7 studies were considered to be at high risk of bias, 4 moderate and 5 low.
- **Conflicting data:** A separate meta-analysis by Nemezio et al. (2017) saw the temperature association only in the subgroup measured rectally, per the Matos and Silva review. An observational study by Un Lam et al. (2016) reported a fever prevalence of 49.9%.

- **Overall trajectory:** A 2026 review says over the first year, total sleep time decreases, nocturnal sleep consolidates with less nocturnal awakenings, and daytime sleep decreases.

  "prompt": "Extract all specific numbers: average minutes of fuss/cry per day by age, colic percentages by country (UK, Canada, Italy, Denmark, Germany, Japan, Netherlands), the number of infants and studies, and any quotes from Dieter Wolke."
}
result
**Crying time (average per 24 hours, from the meta-analysis):**
- First two weeks: about 2 hours
- Peak at six weeks: about 2 hours 15 minutes
- Twelve weeks: about 1 hour 10 minutes
- Individual range: as little as 30 minutes to more than 5 hours

**Colic rates by country:**
- UK: 28% of infants at 1-2 weeks
- Canada: 34.1% at 3-4 weeks
- Italy: 20.9% at 8-9 weeks
- Denmark: 5.5% at 3-4 weeks
- Germany: 6.7% at 3-4 weeks
- Japan and the Netherlands: Listed as study countries, but no colic percentages are given.

**Colic definition:** Crying more than 3 hours a day, at least 3 days a week.

**Sample size:** About 8,700 infants. The number of studies is not stated.

**Dieter Wolke (University of Warwick, lead author):**
- On individual variation, he says: "Babies are already very different in how much they cry in the first weeks of life—there are large but normal variations."
- He also says a new chart of normal crying amounts will help health professionals reassure parents and identify excessive crying that may need further evaluation. (Paraphrased.)

Links: [{"title":"age related incidence curve hospitalized shaken baby syndrome cases","url":"https://www.ojp.gov/ncjrs/virtual-library/abstracts/age-related-incidence-curve-hospitalized-shaken-baby-syndrome-cases"},{"title":"Preventing Shaken Baby Syndrome","url":"https://perspectives.waimh.org/2007/12/15/preventing-shaken-baby-syndrome/"},{"title":"ictes.chmnu.edu.ua","url":"https://ictes.chmnu.edu.ua/?p=22781"},{"title":"4 preventing shaken baby syndrome 07","url":"https://perspectives.waimh.org/2007/12/15/preventing-shaken-baby-syndrome/4-preventing-shaken-baby-syndrome-07"},{"title":"read.qxmd.com","url":"https://read.qxmd.com/doi/10.1016/j.chiabu.2005.06.009"},{"title":"www.ojp.gov","url":"https://www.ojp.gov/taxonomy/term/california?page=44"},{"title":"www.oregon.gov","url":"https://www.oregon.gov:443/oha/PH/DISEASESCONDITIONS/COMMUNICABLEDISEASE/CDSUMMARYNEWSLETTER/Documents/2007/ohd5624.pdf"},{"title":"national center on shaken baby syndrome to implement 1 5 million prevention project","url":"https://www.washington.edu/news/2004/05/26/national-center-on-shaken-baby-syndrome-to-implement-1-5-million-prevention-project/"},{"title":"National Center on Shaken Baby SyndromeNational Center on Shaken Baby Syndrome","url":"https://www.dontshake.org/ispcan"},{"title":"pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov","url":"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16406023"}]

The 2006 paper is Barr, Trent, and Cross in *Child Abuse & Neglect* (vol. 30, pp. 7–16). It used California hospital discharge records from October 1996 to December 2000 and found 273 SBS hospitalizations. The age-specific incidence of SBS began at 2 to 3 weeks of age and declined to baseline by around 36 weeks. The authors reported that the SBS curve peaked later than the normal crying curve. The normal crying curve peaks at 5 to 6 weeks, while SBS hospitalizations peaked at 10 to 13 weeks.

The authors read this as indirect support for crying as a trigger. They concluded that, among the likely predisposing causes, only the properties of early crying fit this pattern. They also offered a reason for the lag: repeat shakings before hospitalization could explain the difference in peak timing. Coverage from the Washington-based National Center on Shaken Baby Syndrome makes the same point, noting that many cases may result from repeated shakings.

The evidence is indirect. A commentary in the WAIMH newsletter notes that excessive crying is often cited as the trigger, yet there are limited data backing this claim. Since the study compares population curves rather than individual cases, it cannot establish that crying caused any particular shaking. Its stated aim was to show that the pattern is consistent with crying as a stimulus, and the authors also said that future research should examine whether crying may be a stimulus for other forms of abuse.

The search didn't return the Isenberg 1998 paper itself, so I can't confirm its findings. It does confirm the citation. A JAMA letter lists the reference as Isenberg SJ, Apt L, McCarty J, et al., "Development of tearing in preterm and term neonates," Arch Ophthalmol. 1998;116773-776 (the page numbers are garbled in the source, but 116:773-776 is the standard citation). That letter also says the study's approach included a topical anesthetic to measure only basal tear secretion, and it reports that term newborns have normal tear secretion and that preterm infants also have the capacity to secrete tears, especially when made to cry, with this function improving with maturity. That is a secondhand description from a letter to the editor, so treat it as indirect.

Related studies that address the same question:

- **Toker et al., 2002** (a different study): It measured Schirmer tear secretion before and after topical anesthesia in 96 term and 22 preterm infants. The mean basal tear production was 7.3 (+/-3.2) mm in term and 3.5 (+/-1.3) mm in preterm infants. The authors concluded that preterm infants have reduced tear secretion compared with term infants.
- **A 2022 abstract (AIOS)**: It reported mean basal tear secretion was 4.2(±3.0) mm in preterm & 8.7(± 3.1) mm in term neonates.

For the Isenberg paper itself, check the Archives of Ophthalmology/JAMA Network listing directly. The source I found was an institutional proxy URL, so it may need library access.

The search turned up the study, but not the mL-per-feed figure you asked for. The paper reports colostrum intake as mass in grams, not volume.

- **Study:** Santoro et al., *Journal of Pediatrics* 2010 (vol. 156, pp. 29-32), on colostrum intake during the first 24 hours in exclusively breastfed healthy newborns. Milk ingested during the first 24 hours of life by 90 healthy newborn infants was evaluated by use of a scale with high sensitivity.
- **Per-feed result:** The mass of colostrum ingested was evaluated in 307 feedings, with 3.4 ± 1 feedings recorded per 8-hour period of observation. Mean gain per feeding was 1.5 ± 1.1 g.
- **Daily total:** The daily mass of milk ingested by newborn infants was estimated at 15 ± 11 g. The authors also reported that this volume did not increase over the first 24 postnatal hours.

Because the paper measured weight gain, the 1.5 g per feed is a mass figure. Converting it to mL would need an assumed colostrum density, which the search results don't provide. The search also didn't show the full text, so I couldn't confirm whether the paper gives a mL conversion. For that, the full article (DOI 10.1016/j.jpeds.2009.07.009) would be needed.

Both sources are in the results, and they agree on one point: the newborn stomach's size is uncertain.

**Bergman 2013 (Acta Paediatrica):** The review's authors searched for studies measuring neonatal stomach capacity and found six articles, which they took to support a capacity of about 20 mL at birth. From that figure they concluded that a term neonate's feeding interval would be roughly 1 hour, and they linked this to human milk gastric emptying time and the neonatal sleep cycle. They also argued that larger volumes at longer intervals might contribute to spitting up, reflux, and hypoglycaemia. Their conclusion is a hypothesis drawn from a small evidence base, not a trial result. ([PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23662739/))

**Spangler 2008 (Journal of Human Lactation):** I could not confirm the authorship from the search results. The SAGE abstract matches the description of a 2008 review of newborn stomach capacity. It found that marble and ball models, which are commonly used to illustrate stomach size, have not been shown to be accurate. The review reported limited data, mostly from the early 1900s, and found that anatomic capacity varies with birth weight. It also concluded that physiologic capacity is unrelated to anatomic capacity and reflects milk production and intake. ([SAGE](https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/abs/10.1177/0890334408316079)) A Science News article attributes a 2008 Journal of Human Lactation review to the same concern: few solid studies exist, and they reach different conclusions. ([Science News](https://www.sciencenews.org/blog/growth-curve/newborn-stomach-size-breastfeeding))

**Discrepancy:** Popular sources often cite a marble holding about 5 to 7 mL, while the 20 mL figure comes from Bergman's review. The Science News piece calls the marble estimate into question and notes that other reviews put the average newborn stomach near 20 mL. ([Science News](https://www.sciencenews.org/blog/growth-curve/newborn-stomach-size-breastfeeding)) Note that the 2013 paper's 20 mL figure applies to a term neonate, and the search returned no direct trial comparing 1-hour feeding with standard 2–4-hour schedules.

The search confirmed both papers exist, but it did not return the full text of either, so I couldn't check their exact figures.

- **Hinde 2009 (Am J Hum Biol 21(4): 512–519):** The title is confirmed in a reference list, which describes sex-biased investment during lactation varying with maternal life history in rhesus macaques ([3-14](https://nutrition.org/lets-hear-girls/)). Press coverage reports that macaque mothers' milk is richer in fat when the baby is male, especially at a first birth, while they produce more milk overall for daughters ([4-7, 4-8](https://www.ksl.com/article/news/entertainment/entertainment-news/animal-moms-customize-milk-depending-on-babys-sex/28716647)). - **Hinde et al. 2014 (PLoS ONE 9(2): e86169), "Holsteins Favor Heifers, Not Bulls":** The title and journal details are confirmed ([9-1, 9-3](https://isearch.asu.edu/profile/2740008)). Coverage says the team analyzed lactation records from nearly 1.5 million Holstein cows, and that cows bearing daughters produced about 1.6% more milk ([4-11, 4-13](https://www.ksl.com/article/news/entertainment/entertainment-news/animal-moms-customize-milk-depending-on-babys-sex/28716647)).

**Discrepancy:** Your query says 1.3%, but the press coverage I found reports 1.6%. I couldn't find the figure in the paper itself, so check the PLoS ONE article directly before relying on either number.

The paper you're looking for is Italianer et al., "Circadian Variation in Human Milk Composition, a Systematic Review," published in Nutrients 2020 (12(8):2328). The search confirmed the main findings but not the direction of the melatonin or cortisol rhythms, so I can't confirm the night-time peak you mentioned from these results.

- **Scope:** The review pooled 83 reports assessing the circadian variation in the concentration of 71 human milk components.
- **Key findings:** The authors report that significant circadian variation is found in tryptophan, fats, triacylglycerol, cholesterol, iron, melatonin, cortisol, and cortisone.
- **Caveats:** The authors note that heterogeneity among studies was high, and the methodological quality varied widely.
- **Methods:** The team searched several databases, and the protocol was registered with PROSPERO (CDR42020187210).

Full text is available via PMC (PMC7468880) and PubMed (PMID 32759654) if you need the melatonin and cortisol specifics.

Links: [{"title":"Changes in immunomodulatory constituents of human milk in response to active infection in the nursing infant","url":"https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22258136/"},{"title":"nature.com","url":"https://www.nature.com/articles/pr201135"},{"title":"Immunomodulatory constituents of human milk change in response to infant bronchiolitis","url":"https://research.sahmri.org.au/en/publications/immunomodulatory-constituents-of-human-milk-change-in-response-to/"},{"title":"journals.pagepress.org","url":"https://journals.pagepress.org/hmr/article/download/448/462/1802"},{"title":"link.springer.com","url":"https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-1-4615-1371-1_61"},{"title":"www.nature.com","url":"https://www.nature.com/articles/pr1989137"},{"title":"Tumor necrosis factor-α in human milk","url":"https://researchexperts.utmb.edu/en/publications/tumor-necrosis-factor-%CE%B1-in-human-milk/"},{"title":"link.springer.com","url":"https://link.springer.com/doi/10.1007/978-1-4757-4242-8_66"},{"title":"Expression of functional immunomodulatory and anti-inflammatory factors in human milk","url":"https://researchexperts.utmb.edu/en/publications/expression-of-functional-immunomodulatory-and-anti-inflammatory-f/"}]

The 2012 paper is Riskin et al., "Changes in immunomodulatory constituents of human milk in response to active infection in the nursing infant," in *Pediatric Research* 71(2):220-5. The study enrolled breast milk from mothers of 31 infants up to 3 months old hospitalized with fever, sampled during illness and recovery, with 20 healthy-infant mothers as controls. Total CD45 leukocyte counts fell from the acute phase to after recovery, and macrophage counts also decreased. TNF-α levels dropped from 3.66 to 2.91 pg/ml. The authors concluded that during active infection, milk white cells (especially macrophages) and TNF-α rise, which they suggest reflects a dynamic protective response. The decrease in lactoferrin was of borderline significance, and no such differences appeared in control samples. Interleukin-10 fell in both sick and healthy groups' milk after recovery, so that finding needs further study. Secretory IgA did not change significantly in either group.

A commentary on the paper (Pediatric Research, linked from nature.com) notes that secretory IgA and lactoferrin, thought to be primary milk defenses, showed no significant response.

**Methods**

- **Women:** 46 pregnant mothers were recruited (39 white, 4 black, 2 Hispanic, 1 Asian). Six more were disqualified. Group sizes are listed as CW n=16, WC n=17, and WW n=14, but Tables 1 and 2 show 15 CW pairs, so the analyzed total is 46.
- **Carrot juice dose:** 300 mL, on 4 days per week, for 3 consecutive weeks in the last trimester (33.2 ± 0.3 weeks gestation), then again during the first 2 months of lactation (1.3 ± 0.1 months).
- **Groups:** CW drank carrot juice in pregnancy and water in lactation. WC drank water in pregnancy and carrot juice in lactation. WW drank water in both periods.
- **Infant testing:** Infants were 5.7 ± 0.2 months old, about 4 (± 0.5) weeks after cereal was introduced. Each infant was tested twice, about 4 days apart, with cereal mixed 1:1 with water or with carrot juice. Sessions were in counterbalanced order and held at the same time of day (10 am to 2 pm).

**Results**

| Measure | Group | Plain | Carrot-flavored | Key statistics |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Negative facial responses | CW | 5.4 ± 0.8 | 4.0 ± 0.8 | Prenatal exposure: paired t[14]=2.88, P=.01 |
| | WC | 4.7 ± 0.8 | 3.4 ± 0.6 | Postnatal exposure: paired t[16]=2.41, P=.03 |
| | WW | 3.6 ± 0.8 | 4.4 ± 0.6 | Not significant (P=.44); group × flavor interaction P=.05 |
| Mothers' enjoyment ratings (1–9) | CW | 5.3 ± 0.6 | 7.5 ± 0.5 | Paired t[14]=3.32, P=.005 |
| | WC | 5.5 ± 0.7 | 6.7 ± 0.5 | Not significant (P=.12) |
| | WW | 5.6 ± 0.7 | 5.2 ± 0.8 | Not significant (P=.70); interaction P=.04 |
| Intake (g) | CW | 55.2 ± 18.2 | 80.5 ± 18.2 | Group × flavor interaction not significant (P=.34) |
| | WC | 63.4 ± 13.2 | 81.4 ± 14.6 | |
| | WW | 34.5 ± 9.7 | 43.9 ± 12.3 | |
| Feeding length (min) | CW | 13.4 ± 1.7 | 18.2 ± 2.2 | Group × flavor interaction not significant (P=.42) |
| | WC | 10.0 ± 1.1 | 13.7 ± 1.6 | |
| | WW | 12.1 ± 2.6 | 13.2 ± 2.4 | |

Additional findings:

- **Proportional scores** (carrot/[carrot + plain], where values above 0.50 mean more of the response for carrot):
  - Negative facial responses: CW 0.39, WC 0.41, WW 0.56.
  - Maternal ratings: CW 0.60, WC 0.56, WW 0.46.
  - Intake: CW 0.62, WC 0.57, WW 0.51.
  - Feeding length: CW 0.57, WC 0.58, WW 0.52.
- **Overall flavor effects across all groups:** Infants consumed more carrot-flavored cereal (F[1,45]=17.15, P=.0001) and fed longer on it (F[1,45]=7.91, P=.007).
- **Correlations with maternal ratings:** intake r=0.54 (P=.000) and feeding length r=0.45 (P=.002).

Here's what the search turned up. I didn't find the Steiner 1979 paper itself, so those points rest on secondary citations.

- **Beauchamp, Cowart & Moran (1986):** The title is "Developmental changes in salt acceptability in human infants," in *Developmental Psychobiology* 19, 17–25 (J., & Moran, M. (1986). Developmental changes in salt acceptability in human infants. Developmental Psychobiology, 19, 17–25.). A review reports that the preference for salty water emerges at about the fourth month of life and is well marked in 6- to 24-month-old infants, and that it appears to decline later, around 31 to 60 months.

- **Steiner (1979):** I could not confirm a 1979 paper. Steiner's early gustofacial work is cited as 1973 and 1974 publications, and a review describes them as illustrations of the facial reactions in newborns evoked by the sweet, salty, sour, and bitter tastes. Reviews also say at birth, infants display a disgust face when exposed to quinine, and that sour taste is rejected at birth by human infants, who display negative mimics after presentation of a sour solution.

- **Rosenstein & Oster (1988):** The 1988 *Child Development* paper is titled "Differential facial responses to four basic tastes in newborns" (vol. 59, pp. 1555–1568). One reference list attributes it to Steiner, but that looks like a citation-formatting error. Reviews cite Rosenstein & Oster for the point that at birth, reactions to an aqueous solution of salt are evasive and spread over neutral reactions to rejection.

- **Other relevant work:** A 2012 prospective study by Stein, Cowart & Beauchamp tested infants (n = 61) at 2 and 6 mo to assess their response to 0.17 and 0.34 mol NaCl/L in water.

I found no source giving 5 cesarean, so check the paper itself before citing that number.
- **2016 Nature Medicine:** The paper is "Partial restoration of the microbiota of cesarean-born infants via vaginal microbial transfer." The team sampled 18 infants and their mothers, including seven born vaginally and 11 delivered by scheduled C-section. Of the C-section-delivered babies, four were exposed to their mothers' vaginal fluids at birth, using gauze that was incubated in the mothers' vaginas for one hour before the C-section. The authors caution that results establish feasibility but not health outcomes.

The search confirms the basic design of the trial but did not return the specific figures you listed (13.7%, 1.9%, 86.1%, 35.3%, 10.6%), so I couldn't verify them.

- **Design and population:** The trial was published in NEJM in 2015 (vol. 372, pp. 803–813). The researchers randomly assigned 640 infants with severe eczema, egg allergy, or both to consume or avoid peanuts until 60 months of age.
- **Cohorts:** Participants were stratified into two study cohorts on the basis of the results of a skin-prick test for peanut allergy. One cohort had no measurable wheal and the other had a wheal of 1 to 4 mm.
- **Headline results:** In the intention-to-treat analysis, 17.2% of the group that avoided peanut developed a positive peanut challenge test at the age of 5 years compared to 3.2% in the group that consumed the food. A secondary account describes the protocol-adherent children as less than 1% of children who consumed peanut as per study protocol and completed the study developing allergy, versus 17.3% in the avoidance group.

The 17.2% vs 3.2% figures are the closest match I found to your numbers. The 13.7%, 1.9%, 86.1%, 35.3%, and 10.6% figures may come from different analysis populations (for example, the full cohort versus the sensitized subgroup), but I couldn't confirm that from these results. Check the abstract and results tables in the full NEJM paper (DOI 10.1056/NEJMoa1414850) before relying on them.

I ran one search. It confirmed the Victora et al. 2015 Pelotas study, but it returned nothing on Colen and Ramey 2014, so I have no sourced information on the sibling-comparison study.

**Victora et al. 2015 (Lancet Global Health)**
- The cohort began in 1982 in Pelotas, Brazil, and the analysis covered the educational achievement and income of 3,493 participants at about age 30.
- Breastfeeding duration was grouped into five categories, and the authors adjusted for 10 "social and biological variables" that might affect IQ. These included family income at birth, parental schooling, maternal smoking during pregnancy, maternal age, birthweight and how the baby was delivered.
- The reported IQ effect was roughly 3.7 points. One source puts it this way: subjects who had been breastfed for 12 months or longer had a higher IQ (about 3.7 points), more years of education and earned roughly 20% more than the average income level. That is close to, but not the same as, your figure of 3.76 points. I did not find the 0.9-year education figure in these results.
- Critics noted gaps. One commentary said the authors did not investigate aspects of the home environment such as parenting style, number of siblings, support with childcare. That point matters for your sibling-comparison question, since sibling designs are one way to address such confounding.

**Colen and Ramey 2014:** No results came back, so I can't summarize it. You would need a targeted search for the paper itself to check its design or findings.

The study checks out, with one discrepancy in the sample sizes for the probability figures.

- **Study:** Lampl and Johnson, published in *Sleep* (2011;34(5):641–650). It used daily parental diaries covering 23 infants over 4 to 17 months, giving 5,798 daily records. Michelle Lampl and Michael L Johnson are the authors, and the title is the one you quoted.
- **Probability figures:** The AASM press release and HealthDay both report the median increase as 43% per additional sleep episode and 20% per additional hour of sleep. HealthDay attributes this to all 23 infants. The investigators also found that the probability of a growth spurt increased by a median of 43 percent for every additional sleep episode and 20 percent for every additional hour of sleep.
- **Discrepancy:** Contemporary Pediatrics gives different denominators, saying the 43% figure came from 12 infants and the 20% figure from 8 infants. That suggests the per-infant analyses did not all use the full sample, so the 23-infant framing applies to the overall study rather than each estimate.
- **Terminology:** Your query says "per sleep bout," but the press materials say "sleep episode." The two terms appear to refer to the same thing in this context.
- **Main finding:** The authors reported that growth spurts in length were temporally linked to bursts of sleep, with growth spurts in body length, which tended to occur within 48 hours of the recorded bursts of sleep.

The search did not return the 1997 paper's abstract, so I could not confirm the specific figures you asked about: the visual cortex peak at about 8 months or the prefrontal peak at about 3.5 years.

What it did confirm:

- **The 1997 paper exists.** It is Huttenlocher and Dabholkar's "Regional differences in synaptogenesis in human cerebral cortex," in *J Comp Neurol* vol. 387, pp. 167–178 (PMID 9336221). The citation appears in a bibliography listing, not in the paper's text. Title: Regional differences in synaptogenesis in human cerebral cortex.
- **Their earlier frontal cortex work.** A 1979 Huttenlocher paper counted synaptic profiles in layer 3 of the middle frontal gyrus across 21 brains. Density increased during infancy, reaching a maximum at age 1–2 years which was about 50% above the adult mean. That peak is earlier than the 3.5-year figure you cited, so the two should not be conflated.
- **Later prefrontal data.** A 2007 Glantz et al. study measured synaptophysin by Western blot in dorsolateral prefrontal cortex. Synaptophysin levels slowly increased from birth until age 5 and then increased more rapidly to peak in late childhood around age 10. This measures protein levels rather than synapse counts, so it is not directly comparable to the Huttenlocher counts.
- **Cross-species comparison.** A 2011 Petanjek et al. paper is cited in the bibliography and deals with prolonged synaptic spine development in human prefrontal cortex. A Genome Research figure also plots human, chimpanzee, and macaque PFC synaptic density and cites the 1997 Huttenlocher and Dabholkar study as a data source. Mean synaptic density per 100 μm2 measured in the PFC of humans (red), chimpanzees (blue), and rhesus macaques (green) at different ages.

To verify the 8-month and 3.5-year figures, the next step is to pull the 1997 abstract directly from PubMed (PMID 9336221) or the journal page.

**What the results do say about Teller acuity and newborns:**
- Teller Acuity Cards are described as a procedure for assessing acuity in newborns and infants, and one 1996 study followed 60 healthy term newborns every three months through their first year. Teller Acuity Cards are a new procedure for evaluation of visual acuity in newborns and infants. We assess by this test 60 healthy term newborns followed up every 3 months from birth to one year of age.
- A 2024 study of preterm infants in a Brazilian NICU reported a mean threshold of 0.32 cycles per degree for preterm infants at around 32 weeks of corrected gestational age.
- A review notes no general agreement on the rate of improvement of acuity over the first two months of life. It adds that some studies found little or no improvement during the first 1-2 postnatal months, while others reported steady gains.
- A Chinese study of infants aged 5 to 24 months reported average binocular acuity rising from 0.17 at 5 months to 0.83 at 24 months (in cycles per degree).

**Bottom line:** These sources support gradual acuity improvement in infancy, with the rate debated for the first two months. They don't confirm the "20/400 at birth" or "adult-like by age" claims, and they don't address accommodation or color vision.

Here's what the search turned up for the three studies:

- **Goren, Sarty & Wu (1975), *Pediatrics*:** This is the most directly confirmed. The study tested 40 newborns with a median age of 9 minutes, and they turned their eyes and heads to follow moving stimuli. Responses were stronger to a proper face pattern than to scrambled versions or a blank (Forty newborn infants, median age 9 minutes, turned their eyes and heads to follow a series of moving stimuli. Responsiveness was significantly greater to a proper face pattern than to either of two scrambled versions of the same stimulus or to a blank.)
- **Replication status:** The finding is contested. A 1991 *Cognition* paper reports replicating the basic result in a second hospital, and extends it to suggest that feature configuration matters in the first hour of life (In Experiment 1 we replicate the basic findings of the study. In Experiment 2 we attempt a second replication in a different maternity hospital). A 1999 study by Easterbrook and colleagues did not replicate it (Although some studies failed to replicate the result (Easterbrook, Kisilevsky, Hains, & Muir, 1999)).
- **Bushnell, Sai & Mullin (1989), *British Journal of Developmental Psychology*:** The search confirmed the citation ("Neonatal recognition of mother's face," vol. 7, pp. 3–15), but returned no content on its methods or findings, so I can't report what it found.

A 2018 *Frontiers in Psychology* review cites it for the claim that newborns demonstrate visual preferences for: open-eyes compared to closed-eyes, and for direct over averted eye gaze. That is secondhand, and I didn't verify the PNAS details.

Overall, the search confirmed the 1975 study's details and its contested replication history. It confirmed the 1989 citation only, and it gave secondhand support for the 2002 direct-gaze finding.

I ran one search covering the first two studies. It did not return anything on the 2013 Moon, Lagercrantz, and Kuhl paper, so that study is unverified here.

**DeCasper & Fifer (1980), *Science*:** The study tested ten newborns under three days old. Sucking pauses on a wired nipple chose which recording played, either the baby's own mother reading a story or another mother reading the same story. Eight of the ten shifted their sucking pattern in the direction that produced their own mother's voice. One review adds that the infants had no more than 12 h postnatal experience with their mother, which is why the result is taken to suggest learning before birth.

**DeCasper & Spence (1986):** Pregnant women read the same passage aloud each day for the last six weeks of pregnancy, often from *The Cat in the Hat*. After birth, the babies preferred the rhythm of the passage their mothers had read before they were born. They also preferred it even when a stranger read it, not only their own mother.

**Moon, Lagercrantz & Kuhl (2013), *Acta Paediatrica*:** No results. I could not confirm the sample of 40 infants, the Sweden/US setting, or the vowel findings. Those details need a separate search or a check against the paper itself.

Both studies check out. The 1994 Lancet paper reports that of 30 infants, 22 spontaneously selected the unwashed breast. The authors concluded that the infants responded to olfactory differences between the washed and unwashed breasts.

The 2001 Acta Paediatrica paper is titled "Breast odour as only maternal stimulus elicits crawling towards the odour source" (Varendi & Porter, vol. 90, pp. 372–375). A cotton pad bearing the mother's breast odor was placed 17 cm in front of a prone baby on a warm bed. The odor prompted crawling toward the pad and typical pre-feeding behaviors. The authors concluded that smell, in the absence of other maternal cues, helps guide the baby to the nipple and evokes the behavioral preludes of breastfeeding.

Notes:
- The 2001 paper's title differs slightly from your query's wording, but the study matches the description.
- One source gives the Lancet pages as 3–4 rather than 989–90. The PubMed record and the thesis list 989–990, so that is likely the correct citation.
- A later follow-up (Acta Paediatrica 1997) found that on days 3-4 significantly more babies still selected their mother's unwashed breast (n = 21) than the washed alternative (n = 8).

The 2015 eLife paper by Goksan et al. is confirmed, but the search results don't support the "10 infants" or "threshold four times lower" details, so I can't verify those.

- **Core finding:** The study used fMRI to compare newborns and adults after acute noxious stimulation. Infants showed activity in 18 of the 20 adult brain regions, but not in the amygdala or orbitofrontal cortex (Using fMRI we identified the network of brain regions that are active following acute noxious stimulation in newborn infants, and compared the activity to that observed in adults. Significant infant brain activity was observed in 18 of the 20 active adult brain regions but not in the infant amygdala or orbitofrontal cortex.)
- **Stimulus:** The comparison used a sharp poke of the foot, with adults also reporting their pain (a sharp poke of the foot).
- **Interpretation:** The authors concluded that infants likely experience pain in ways similar to adults, though possibly without some of the emotional components, and that appropriate pain relief matters (infants experience pain in similar ways to adults, though they may not experience all the emotions that adults have when they are in pain. It is, therefore, important to give infants suitable pain relief during potentially painful procedures.)

Sources: eLife article (elifesciences.org/articles/6356), PMC4402596, and the Oxford and UCL repository pages. The results didn't include sample size or the pain threshold figure, so those would need checking against the full paper.

Here's what the search turned up for the two studies. Neither search result confirmed the exact sample details you asked about (the 29-day age, or the 2011 *Psychological Science* publication), so those remain unverified.

**Meltzoff & Borton (1979), *Nature*, "Intermodal matching by human neonates"**
- The paper's abstract is only partly visible, and it covers adults judging shape across touch and vision. Normal human adults judge two identical objects to have the same shape even when they are perceived through different modalities, such as touch and vision.
- A later critique describes the design: infants explored a pacifier tactually, then saw a visual pair. Infants looked longer at a matching shape than at a novel shape with which it was paired. The same critique reports the age as 1 month, not 29 days.
- Replication has been mixed. A 1999 study by Maurer and colleagues found there is not good evidence that 1-month-olds can transfer information about these shapes from touch to vision.

**Wagner & Dobkins (2011), synaesthetic associations in infants**
- Coverage of the study says infants who were two and three months old showed significant shape-color associations. By eight months the preference was no longer pronounced, and in adults it was gone altogether.
- The design involved looking-time differences: some infants might look significantly longer at a green background with circles than at the same green background with triangles.
- Karen Dobkins suggested that all infants are born with a lot of crosstalk between the different modules.

I did not find a primary source for the 2011 paper itself, so the journal and the exact methods should be checked against the original article.

The search didn't turn up the Miller and Reedy studies, and it didn't find anything on infant taste bud counts, so I can't confirm the 1990/2000 figures or the infant claim from these results.

What the results do show:

- **The 10,000 figure is widely repeated.** A Monell Chemical Senses Center scientist, quoted on a Science Update segment, said most people have about 10,000 taste buds. He consulted biological scientist Gary Beauchamp of the Monell Chemical Senses Center. He says most people have about 10,000 taste buds.
- **Lower estimates also appear.** One source puts adults at between 2,000 to 4,000 taste buds in total. Another says it is a common misconception that adults have exactly 10,000 taste buds, the reality is a much wider range, typically between 2,000 and 4,000. These sources are consumer-oriented sites and don't cite primary research, so treat them with caution.
- **Other sources give a middle range.** MedicineNet says the average tongue has somewhere between 5,000 and 10,000 taste buds.
- **Aging may reduce the count.** WebMD notes that as you age, some taste buds stop regrowing, so older people may have closer to 5,000 working taste buds.
- **Papillae vs. taste buds.** Taste buds sit inside papillae. One source notes that fungiform papillae are the most common and are found mostly at the tip of the tongue and at the edges. Those papillae are where the fungiform taste buds are, which is relevant to how Miller and Reedy's counts would be framed.

Bottom line: sources range from about 2,000 to 10,000 for adults, with 10,000 being the most common claim. I found no reliable source for the infant figure, and the Miller and Reedy papers would need a direct search of the literature to verify.

Links: [{"title":"↓ Skip to main content","url":"https://cochrane.altmetric.com/details/14055381"},{"title":"CD003519 es abstract","url":"https://www.cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.CD003519.pub4/pdf/CDSR/CD003519/CD003519_es_abstract.pdf"},{"title":"skin to skin contact improves breastfeeding of healthy babies","url":"https://evidence.nihr.ac.uk/alert/skin-to-skin-contact-improves-breastfeeding-of-healthy-babies/"},{"title":"Scolaris Content Display Scolaris Content Display","url":"https://cochranelibrary.com/es/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.cd003519.pub4/information"},{"title":"Scolaris Content Display Scolaris Content Display","url":"https://cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.cd003519.pub4/information"},{"title":"immediate or early skin to skin contact for mothers and their hea","url":"https://researchers.westernsydney.edu.au/en/publications/immediate-or-early-skin-to-skin-contact-for-mothers-and-their-hea/"},{"title":"Portal de Boas Práticas em Saúde da Mulher, da Criança e do Adolescente","url":"https://portaldeboaspraticas.iff.fiocruz.br/?p=5937"},{"title":"Portal de Boas Práticas em Saúde da Mulher, da Criança e do Adolescente","url":"https://portaldeboaspraticas.iff.fiocruz.br/biblioteca/early-skin%e2%80%90to%e2%80%90skin-contact-for-mothers-and-their-healthy-newborn-infants/"},{"title":"Portal de Boas Práticas em Saúde da Mulher, da Criança e do Adolescente","url":"https://portaldeboaspraticas.iff.fiocruz.br/biblioteca/early-skin%E2%80%90to%E2%80%90skin-contact-for-mothers-and-their-healthy-newborn-infants"},{"title":"You are here","url":"https://jordantimes.com/node/288477"}]

The 2016 Cochrane review is Moore, Bergman, Anderson and Medley, "Early skin-to-skin contact for mothers and their healthy newborn infants" (CD003519, published November 2016). The results I found don't include the review's abstract or its exact RR, so I can't confirm the 1.24 figure from the primary source.

- **Breastfeeding at 1–4 months:** Press coverage of the review reports roughly a 24% higher likelihood of still breastfeeding at one to four months for infants with immediate skin-to-skin contact versus usual care. That matches the RR of 1.24 in your query. The NIHR plain-English summary describes about a quarter more women with this contact still breastfeeding at one to four months.
- **Blood glucose:** Reuters reported that infants with skin-to-skin contact had higher blood sugar levels. However, the NIHR summary says the evidence on blood sugar and physiological stability was based on fewer trials and less strong.
- **Limitations:** NIHR notes it remains unclear if the benefits of skin-to-skin contact are equally seen after caesarean birth, if duration of contact influences the outcome and if physiological stability of babies is improved.
- **Source status:** The NIHR summary notes it was posted more than 5 years ago. More recent research findings may have been published.

For the exact effect sizes and confidence intervals, check the Cochrane Library record at DOI 10.1002/14651858.CD003519.pub4.

The search supports most of your figures, but not the "one eighth" head ratio.

- **Head proportion:** Textbook-style sources say the head is about 25% of body length at birth. They put it at about 15% in adulthood, not one eighth. At birth, the head makes up about 25 percent of a person's length. In adulthood, the head comprises about 15 percent of a person's length. A Tresillian parenting page gives a similar birth figure, one quarter of total body length. At birth, the head is approximately one-quarter of the baby's total body length, while the legs only take-up one-third of the total body length.
- **Weight:** Most sources say weight doubles around 4 months and triples by 12 months. One parenting source widens the doubling window to 4–6 months. Birth weight often doubles by 4–6 months and triples by 12 months although this is impacted by many things including genetics.
- **Length:** The textbook figures are an average newborn length of 19.5 inches and 29.5 inches at 12 months. That is roughly a 50% gain. The average length of a newborn is 19.5 inches, increasing to 29.5 inches by 12 months and 34.4 inches by 2 years old. Tresillian describes the first-year gain as usually 1.5 times in 12 months.

Sources: the Lumen/OpenStax-style child development text (libretexts/lumenlearning) and Tresillian. The 15% adult head figure is the most common one in these textbooks; the 1/8 figure didn't appear in any result.

result
**Study:** University of Iowa researchers, published in *Current Biology* (2021)

- **Journal and first author:** *Current Biology*; first author Greta Sokoloff, with Mark S. Blumberg among the authors.
- **Sample:** 22 sleeping infants, aged one week to seven months.
- **Twitching in REM vs. quiet sleep:** Twitching was already known during REM sleep. The researchers also found twitches during quiet sleep, which had not been expected. The twitches appeared similar in both stages.
- **When it increases:** Twitching during quiet sleep rises sharply beginning around three months of age.
- **Brain activity:** Infants produced sleep spindles about once every 10 seconds during quiet sleep. Spindle rates rose from about three to seven months, concentrated in the sensorimotor cortex. Spindles and twitches were synchronized.
- **Interpretation:** Sokoloff suggested infants may be learning about their bodies through twitching, since spindles are linked to learning and memory.
- **Blumberg's quotes:**
  - On the surprise: "This was completely surprising and, for all we know, unique to humans and human infants,"
  - On brain-body integration: "Infants have to integrate the brain with the body, to get the system set up and working properly."
- **Limitations:** The sample is small, especially for younger infants, and recordings covered only short daytime naps. The researchers plan larger studies covering day and night sleep.
- **Funding:** National Institute of Child Health and Human Development (NIH).

result
**Key findings and numbers**

- **Species:** Newborn rats. The study compared rat whisker twitches to human REM-sleep eye movements.
- **Ages:** Newborn, or "pups." The article gives no more specific ages.
- **Twitch rate:** The interviewer says Blumberg estimated that limb muscles twitch at least 40,000 times a day. The article does not report a whisker twitch rate.
- **Brain regions:** The thalamus, which receives whisker input, and the cortex, which was studied through imaging.
- **Core finding:** Whisker twitches during sleep are linked to bursts of brain activity that are typically not seen when the rats are awake. Sleep twitches appear to play a role in early brain development, not just to be dream remnants.
- **Journal:** *Current Biology*
- **First author:** Alex Tiriac, third-year psychology graduate student
- **Second author:** Brandt Uitermarkt, second-year psychology graduate student
- **Senior author/lab lead:** Mark Blumberg, F. Wendell Miller Professor of Psychology and Biology at the University of Iowa

**Quotes from Mark Blumberg**

- Sleep twitches were long considered "functionless by-products of a dreaming brain."
- He says the popular idea "can't be correct—certainly can't be completely correct," because animals still twitch after parts of the brain critical for dreaming are removed.
- "Whisker twitching during sleep drives brain activity," similar to what has been seen with limb twitching.
- "This tells us that not only are the whiskers twitching, but the brain is paying attention to it."
